The edge is on the path iff j is on the path and i is a child of j on the path. In other words, the path from u to v goes through j and i consecutively, with i being the child. This depends on the rest of the tree.

Perhaps we can condition on the tree restricted to vertices {1,2,...,M} for some M, but i is up to N. This seems messy.

Alternative: Since the sum is over all P, and for each P we take the tree, maybe we can swap the sum: Sum over P of distance(u,v) = Sum over P of sum_{e in path} w(e). Swap sums: = Sum_{i=2}^N A_i * (number of P such that edge (i, P_i) is on the u–v path). So we need for each i, count of parent choices P_i such that the edge (i, P_i) lies on the u–v path. Let's fix i. For which j (1≤j<i) does the edge (j,i) lie on the u–v path? This depends on the rest of the tree, because the path depends on all parent choices. But maybe for a fixed i and j, the probability (over random P) that the edge (j,i) is on the u–v path is independent of other choices? Since the model is random recursive tree, we can compute the probability that the edge between j and i is on the path between u and v. Then multiply by total number of trees (N-1)! to get the count. So the answer is (N-1)! * Σ_{i=2}^N A_i * P_{u,v}(i), where P_{u,v}(i) is the probability that in the random recursive tree, the edge (parent(i),i) is on the u–v path. This seems more tractable.

So we need to compute for each query (u,v) the sum: (N-1)! * Σ_i A_i * p_i(u,v), where p_i(u,v) = Prob( edge (parent(i), i) ∈ path(u,v) ).

We can precompute for all pairs? N and Q up to 2e5, so we need something subquadratic.

Let's find p_i(u,v). In a random recursive tree, what is the probability that the edge between i and its parent is on the path between u and v?

Observation: The random recursive tree has the property that the ancestor relationship is random. For any two vertices a,b, the probability that a is an ancestor of b is 1/(b-1) if a<b, and 0 if a>b? No, if a>b, a cannot be ancestor of b because ancestor must have smaller label? Wait, in recursive tree, labels increase along any root-to-leaf path. So if a is ancestor of b, then a < b. Conversely, if a < b, a could be ancestor of b, but not necessarily. The probability that a is ancestor of b (for a<b) is 1/(b-1). This is a known fact.

For three vertices, there are more complex probabilities. We need the probability that the edge (i, parent(i)) is on path(u,v). This is equivalent to: in the tree, i is on the path, and parent(i) is the next vertex on the path toward the root. This means that parent(i) is an ancestor of i, and the path from u to v goes through parent(i) and i. In other words, the LCA of u and v is either parent(i) or an ancestor of parent(i), and the path from u to v includes the edge between i and parent(i). More specifically, the edge (parent(i),i) is on the u–v path iff one of the following holds:
1. u is in the subtree of i (i.e., i is ancestor of u), and v is not in the subtree of i, and the path from i to v goes up through parent(i). Wait, if i is ancestor of u, then the path from u to v goes up from u to i, then up to parent(i), etc. The edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path if the path from u to v goes up from i to parent(i). That happens iff v is not in the subtree of i (i.e., i is not ancestor of v). So if i is ancestor of u but not v, then the path from u to v goes up through parent(i), so the edge is used.
2. Similarly, if i is ancestor of v but not u, then the path from v to u goes up through parent(i), so the edge is used.
3. If i is ancestor of both u and v, then the path between u and v is within the subtree of i, so it does not use the edge to parent(i).
4. If i is not ancestor of u or v, then the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path only if the path from u to v goes through i and its parent. That means that the LCA of u and v is on the path from i to parent(i)? Actually, if i is not ancestor of u or v, then the path from u to v might go through i if i is on the path. But i is not ancestor of u or v, so i is not on the paths from u to root or v to root, except if i is the LCA or between them. The path from u to v goes up from u to L, then down to v. For i to be on this path, i must be on the u→L segment or the L→v segment. But if i is not ancestor of u or v, can i be on the path? Yes, if i is on the u→L segment, then i is an ancestor of u? No, on the u→L segment, vertices are ancestors of u. Because L is ancestor of u, and the path from u to L consists of ancestors of u. Similarly, the L→v segment consists of ancestors of v. So any vertex on the u–v path is an ancestor of u or an ancestor of v (or both, if it's L). So i is on the path iff i is an ancestor of u or an ancestor of v (or both). More precisely, the set of vertices on the path is exactly the union of ancestors of u and ancestors of v, minus the ancestors of L (since L is included in both). Actually, ancestors of u include L and above; ancestors of v include L and above. The union is ancestors of u ∪ ancestors of v. The path is (ancestors of u \ ancestors of L) ∪ {L} ∪ (ancestors of v \ ancestors of L). But L is included. So a vertex i is on the path iff i is an ancestor of u or an ancestor of v.

Now, the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path iff i is on the path and the path uses the edge to parent. If i is on the path, when does the path use the edge to parent? The path uses the edge to parent iff the path goes from i to parent(i) (i.e., toward the root). This happens if i is on the v-side of the path? Let's see: The path goes from u up to L, then down to v. For a vertex i on the path, if i is on the u→L segment (including u, excluding L), then the path from i to v goes down to L then down to v, so it goes away from root, i.e., from i to its child toward v, not to parent. So the edge to parent is not used. If i is on the L→v segment (including v, excluding L), then the path from i to u goes up to L then up to u, so it goes toward root, i.e., from i to parent(i). So the edge to parent is used. If i = L, the path goes both ways, but the edge to parent is not used (unless L = u, in which case i is on the u→L segment with u=L, but then the segment is just L, and the edge to parent is not used because the path from L to v goes down). Wait, if L = u, then the path is from u down to v. Then for i on the path, if i = u, the edge to parent is not used. For i ≠ u (i on the down segment), the edge to parent is used. So in all cases, the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path iff i is on the path and i is not the "top" vertex (the one closest to root), i.e., i is not L unless L = v? Actually, when L is the meeting point, it's the top. When u is ancestor of v, the top is u. When v is ancestor of u, top is v. So the top is min(u,v) in terms of depth? It's the one with smaller depth among u and v, which is the ancestor of the other, or the LCA.

Thus, edge (i,parent(i)) on path iff i is on the path and i ≠ top, where top = L if L≠u,v? Actually, if u is ancestor of v, top = u. If v is ancestor of u, top = v. Else top = L. In all cases, top is the unique vertex on the path that is an ancestor of the other endpoint? Not exactly: if u ancestor of v, then u is ancestor of v. If v ancestor of u, v is ancestor of u. Else, L is ancestor of both, but not the other way. The top is the vertex among the path that is an ancestor of all other vertices on the path. So edge is on path iff i is on path and i is not an ancestor of some other vertex on the path? More simply, i is on path and i ≠ top.

Therefore, p_i(u,v) = Prob( i is on path(u,v) and i ≠ top(u,v) ). But note that if i = top, then i is on path but edge not used. So p_i = Prob(i on path) - Prob(i = top). But i = top is a specific event.

However, the event "i = top" is a specific case. Maybe we can compute Prob(i on path and edge used) directly.

Let's characterize when the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path. As argued, this happens iff:
- Case A: i is ancestor of u but not v. Then the path from u to v goes up from u to i, then up to parent(i), etc. The edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path. Also, i must be on the path, which it is.
- Case B: i is ancestor of v but not u. Symmetric.
- Case C: i is not ancestor of u or v, but i is on the path. Is that possible? As argued, any vertex on the path is an ancestor of u or v. So if i is not ancestor of u or v, i cannot be on the path. Wait, is that true? In a rooted tree, the path between u and v consists of ancestors of u and ancestors of v. Specifically, the path is the union of the path from u to root and the path from v to root, with the common part (ancestors of L) included once. The vertices on the path are exactly the vertices that are ancestors of u or ancestors of v. So yes, every vertex on the path is an ancestor of u or an ancestor of v (or both). Therefore, i is on the path iff i is an ancestor of u or i is an ancestor of v.

Thus, the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path iff:
- i is ancestor of u xor i is ancestor of v (i.e., exactly one of them), OR
- i is ancestor of both u and v but the edge is still on path? If i is ancestor of both, then i is on the path (it's an ancestor of L or above L). But if i is ancestor of both, then i is in the common part. The path from u to v goes down from i to L, then down to v? Actually, if i is ancestor of both, then i is on the path from u to root and v to root. The path between u and v is the union. If i is above L, then the path from u to v goes up from u to i, then down to v. The edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path only if the path goes from i to parent(i), i.e., if the path uses the edge upward from i. But the path from u to v goes from u up to i, then down to v. It uses the edge (i,parent(i)) only if the path continues upward from i, which happens only if v is not in the subtree of i? Wait, if i is ancestor of both u and v, then the path from u to v goes up from u to i, then down from i to v. It does not go up from i to parent(i) unless v is not in the subtree of i. But if i is ancestor of v, then v is in the subtree of i. So the path goes down from i to v, not up. Therefore, the edge (i,parent(i)) is NOT on the path if i is ancestor of both u and v. Similarly, if i is ancestor of neither, not on path. So the only case is when i is ancestor of exactly one of u,v.

Wait, what about the case where i is the LCA? If i = L, then i is ancestor of both u and v. The edge (i,parent(i)) is not on the path (unless L = u or L = v, but then it's ancestor of the other, not both? If L = u, then u is ancestor of v, but not ancestor of u? u is ancestor of itself. Usually ancestor includes the vertex itself. So if L = u, then i = u is ancestor of u and v. But in this case, the edge (i,parent(i)) is not on the path, as we saw. So indeed, the condition is: i is ancestor of exactly one of u and v (i.e., i is an ancestor of u but not v, or vice versa).

Is that sufficient? If i is ancestor of u but not v, then the path from u to v goes up from u to i (since i is ancestor of u), then up from i to parent(i), etc., until it reaches L (which is above i). Since i is not ancestor of v, v is not in the subtree of i, so the path from i to v must go upward, passing through parent(i). Thus the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path. Similarly if i is ancestor of v but not u.

Therefore, the event "edge (i,parent(i)) is on path(u,v)" is exactly the event that i is an ancestor of exactly one of u and v.

Wow, that's a huge simplification! Let's double-check with examples.
Example 1: u=2, v=3, tree 1-2,1-3. For i=2: ancestor of 2? Yes. Ancestor of 3? No. So exactly one: true. Edge on path? Yes. For i=3: ancestor of 2? No. Ancestor of 3? Yes. Exactly one: true. Edge on path? Yes. For i=1: ancestor of 2? Yes. Ancestor of 3? Yes. Both: false. Edge? No. Good.
Example 2: u=2, v=4, tree 1-2,2-3,2-4. u=2, v=4. For i=2: ancestor of 2? Yes. Ancestor of 4? Yes (2 is parent of 4). Both: false. Edge on path? The path is 2-4, edge is (4,2). Edge (2,1) is not on path. So correct. For i=4: ancestor of 2? No. Ancestor of 4? Yes. Exactly one: true. Edge (4,2) is on path. Yes. For i=3: ancestor of 2? No. Ancestor of 4? No. Neither: false. Edge not on path. Good.
Example 3: u=4, v=5, tree 1-2,1-3,2-4,3-5. u=4, v=5. For i=2: ancestor of 4? Yes. Ancestor of 5? No. Exactly one: true. Edge (2,1) on path? Path is 4-2-1-3-5. Edges: 4-2, 2-1, 1-3, 3-5. (2,1) is on path. Yes. For i=3: ancestor of 4? No. Ancestor of 5? Yes. Exactly one: true. Edge (3,1) on path? Yes. For i=4: ancestor of 4? Yes. Ancestor of 5? No. Exactly one: true. Edge (4,2) on path? Yes. For i=5: ancestor of 4? No. Ancestor of 5? Yes. Exactly one: true. Edge (5,3) on path? Yes. For i=1: ancestor of both. False. Edge (1,none) not on path. Good.

Thus, the probability we need is: p_i(u,v) = Prob( i is ancestor of exactly one of u,v ).

In the random recursive tree, for fixed i, u, v, what is the probability that i is an ancestor of u but not v, plus probability that i is ancestor of v but not u? By symmetry, if u and v are distinct and neither is i, this is 2 * Prob( i is ancestor of u and not ancestor of v )? Not exactly symmetric if u and v have different relationships to i. But we can compute directly.

We need Prob( i is ancestor of u ) and Prob( i is ancestor of v ), and also the joint probability that i is ancestor of both, to get the exclusive probability. So p_i(u,v) = P(anc(i,u)) + P(anc(i,v)) - 2 P(anc(i,u) and anc(i,v)).

But wait, is that correct? P(anc(i,u) xor anc(i,v)) = P(anc(i,u)) + P(anc(i,v)) - 2 P(anc(i,u) ∧ anc(i,v)). Yes.

Now, in the random recursive tree, for i < u, P(i is ancestor of u) = 1/(u-1). For i > u, it's 0. Similarly for v. What about the joint probability? For three distinct vertices a<b<c, the probability that a is ancestor of b and a is ancestor of c? Since being ancestor of b and c is equivalent to being ancestor of b and c being in the subtree of a. The probability that a is ancestor of both b and c is known? We need P( i is ancestor of u and i is ancestor of v ). Assume without loss that i is the smallest among i, u, v? Not necessarily. Let's consider cases based on the order of i, u, v.

Case 1: i is not the smallest. If i > u or i > v, then i cannot be ancestor of that vertex (since ancestor must have smaller label). So if i > u, P(anc(i,u)) = 0. So the joint is 0. If i > u and i > v, then p_i = 0. If i > u but i < v, then P(anc(i,u))=0, P(anc(i,v))=1/(v-1), and joint=0, so p_i = 1/(v-1). But is that correct? For example, i=5, u=3, v=7. i=5 cannot be ancestor of 3. It can be ancestor of 7 with prob 1/6. If it is ancestor of 7, is it ancestor of 3? No, because 3<5, so 3 is not in subtree of 5. So we only need to consider i < u and i < v for non-zero contributions. If i is less than both u and v, then i could be ancestor of both, one, or none. So assume i < u and i < v. Also need to consider if i = u or i = v, but i ranges 2..N, and u,v are given, i could equal u or v. If i = u, then anc(i,u) is true (a vertex is ancestor of itself? In the path, we considered i on path. If i = u, is i ancestor of u? Usually ancestor includes the vertex itself. But does the edge (i,parent(i)) lie on the path when i = u? If i = u, then anc(i,u) is true, anc(i,v) is false (unless i is also ancestor of v, but if i=u and u is not ancestor of v, then anc(i,v)=false). So p_i = 1. Is that correct? For a fixed tree, if i=u, does the edge (u,parent(u)) lie on the u–v path? It lies on the path if u is not the LCA. If u is the LCA (i.e., u is ancestor of v), then the edge is not on the path. If u is not ancestor of v, then anc(i,v) is false, so exclusive is true. So p_u = Prob( u is not ancestor of v ) = 1 - 1/(v-1) (since u<v, prob u is ancestor of v is 1/(v-1)). Similarly for i=v. If i=v, p_v = Prob( v is not ancestor of u ) = 1 - 1/(u-1)? But v>u, so prob v is ancestor of u is 0. Actually, v>u, so v cannot be ancestor of u. So p_v = 1. Wait, if i=v, then anc(i,v) is true, anc(i,u) is false (since v>u). So exclusive is always true? But does the edge (v,parent(v)) lie on the path? As we saw, for i=v, the edge is always on the path because v is on the v-side. Indeed, if v is the endpoint, the path goes from u to v, and the last edge is (v,parent(v)) unless v is the root, but v≥2. So yes, p_v = 1. Let's check: i=v, then anc(i,v) true, anc(i,u) false (since v>u, cannot be ancestor). So exclusive always true. So p_v = 1. That matches: for any tree, the edge incident to v is on the path. Good.

Similarly, for i=u, anc(i,u) true. anc(i,v) is true iff u is ancestor of v. So exclusive is true iff u is not ancestor of v. So p_u = 1 - 1/(v-1) (since u<v). This matches the event that the edge from u to its parent is on the path, which happens unless u is ancestor of v.

So for i = u, p_i = 1 - 1/(v-1). For i = v, p_i = 1.

But wait, the problem statement says A_i is given for i=2..N. So A_u and A_v are defined if u,v ≥2. If u=1, then A_1 is not defined. But distance from 1 to v: path is 1→...→v. The edges are (i,parent(i)) for i on path except 1. So for i=1, no A_1. So we only sum over i≥2. If u=1, then for i=v, p_v=1. For i on path from 1 to v, i is descendant of 1. Then i is ancestor of v? If i is on path from 1 to v, then i is ancestor of v. And i is ancestor of 1? No, since 1 is root, i is not ancestor of 1 (unless i=1, but i≥2). So for i on path from 1 to v (i≠1), anc(i,1)=false, anc(i,v)=true. So exclusive true. So p_i = 1 for all i on path. But the sum over trees of distance(1,v) = sum_i A_i for i on path. Since there are (N-1)! trees, and for each tree, the set of i on path is random, the expected distance is sum A_i * P(i on path). But from our formula, P(i on path and edge used) = P(i ancestor of exactly one of 1,v). Since 1 is root, anc(i,1) is false for all i>1. anc(i,v) is true iff i is ancestor of v. So P(i on path and edge used) = P(i is ancestor of v). This is 1/(v-1) for i<v, and 1 for i=v? Wait, for i=v, anc(v,v) true, so P=1. For i<v, P(anc(i,v)) = 1/(v-1). So sum_{i<v} A_i * 1/(v-1) + A_v * 1. But is that correct? Let's test with N=2, A_2=100, u=1, v=2. Only tree: P_2=1. Distance(1,2)=A_2=100. Sum=100. Our formula: (N-1)! = 1. Sum = A_2 * 1 = 100. Good.

Test N=3, A_2=A_3=1. Trees: (1,1): distances 1-2:1, 1-3:1. (1,2): 1-2:1, 1-3:2. Sum for (1,2): 1+2=3. Sum for (1,3): 1+1=2? Wait sample: 1-2 sum 2, 1-3 sum 3. Let's compute using formula for u=1, v=2: Sum = (N-1)! * (A_2 * P(anc(2,2)) + A_3 * P(anc(3,2)))? Wait, for u=1, v=2: i=2: P(anc(2,2)) = 1. i=3: P(anc(3,2)) = 0 (since 3>2). So sum = 2! * (A_2 * 1) = 2*1=2. Good. For u=1, v=3: i=2: P(anc(2,3)) = 1/(3-1)=1/2. i=3: P(anc(3,3))=1. So sum = 2 * (1 * 1/2 + 1 * 1) = 2*(1.5)=3. Good. So the formula works.

Now for general u,v. We need to compute for each i (2≤i≤N) the probability p_i(u,v) = P( anc(i,u) xor anc(i,v) ). Then the answer for query (u,v) is (N-1)! * Σ_{i=2}^N A_i * p_i(u,v), modulo 998244353.

We need to handle i=u, i=v, i<u<v, i<v<u, i>u and i>v (then p_i=0), etc.

Let's derive p_i(u,v) for all cases.

Let i, u, v be distinct? They may not be distinct; the problem says u_i < v_i, so distinct.

We need P( anc(i,u) ) and P( anc(i,v) ), and the joint.

First, recall the random recursive tree model: vertices 1..N. For i>j, P(anc(i,j)) = 1/(j-1)? Wait, earlier I said for i<j, P(anc(i,j)) = 1/(j-1). Let's verify: For a=2, b=3, P(anc(2,3)) = 1/2? In N=3, trees: (1,1): 2 parent 1, 3 parent 1. 2 is not ancestor of 3. (1,2): 2 parent 1, 3 parent 2. 2 is ancestor of 3. So prob 1/2. Yes, 1/(3-1)=1/2. For a=2, b=4 in N=4, there are 6 trees. How many have 2 ancestor of 4? 2 is ancestor of 4 if parent(4)=2, or parent(4)=1 and 2 is on path? Actually 2 is ancestor of 4 iff 2 is on the path from 4 to root, and 2<4. The probability is 1/3? I think it's 1/(4-1)=1/3. Yes. So for i < j, P(anc(i,j)) = 1/(j-1). For i > j, P(anc(i,j)) = 0 (since labels increase along root path, so ancestor must have smaller label). For i = j, P(anc(i,i)) = 1.

Now, the joint probability P( anc(i,u) and anc(i,v) ) for distinct u,v and i distinct from both? We need to consider the order of i, u, v. There are several cases.

Case 1: i is not the smallest. If i > u, then P(anc(i,u)) = 0. So joint is 0. Similarly if i > v, P(anc(i,v)) = 0. So joint = 0.
Thus, if i > max(u,v), p_i = 0.
If u < i < v (assuming u<v), then P(anc(i,u)) = 0, P(anc(i,v)) = 1/(v-1). Joint = 0. So p_i = 1/(v-1).
Similarly, if v < i < u (impossible since u<v), but if v < i < u? But u<v, so not possible. So only the case i < u < v or i < v < u (i.e., i is the smallest).

Case 2: i is the smallest among {i,u,v}. That is, i < u and i < v. Then i could be ancestor of u, of v, both, or neither. We need P(anc(i,u) and anc(i,v)).

For i < u < v, what is the probability that i is ancestor of both u and v? This is the probability that u and v are both in the subtree of i. In the random recursive tree, the subtree of i is a random recursive tree on the set of vertices that are descendants of i. The descendants of i are a random subset, and given the subset, the tree structure is a random recursive tree. The probability that both u and v are in the subtree of i is: first, u is a descendant of i with probability 1/(u-1) (as above). Given that u is a descendant, what is the probability that v is also a descendant? This might be more complex. Alternatively, there is a known result for random recursive trees: for i < u < v, the probability that i is ancestor of u and v is 1/((u-1)(v-1))? Let's check with small N.

N=3, i=2, u=3, v=? But v must be >u, but N=3 only 3 vertices. For N=4, i=2, u=3, v=4. Let's compute manually. Trees on 4 vertices: 6 trees? Actually (N-1)! = 6. List them:
P = (1,1,1): 1-2,1-3,1-4. 2 not ancestor of 3 or 4.
(1,1,2): 1-2,1-3,2-4. 2 is ancestor of 4, not 3. Both? No.
(1,1,3): 1-2,1-3,3-4. 2 not ancestor.
(1,2,1): 1-2,2-3,1-4. 2 ancestor of 3, not 4. Both? No.
(1,2,2): 1-2,2-3,2-4. 2 ancestor of both 3 and 4. Yes.
(1,2,3): 1-2,2-3,3-4. 2 ancestor of 3, not 4. No.
So only 1 tree out of 6 has i=2 ancestor of both 3 and 4. Probability = 1/6. Now, 1/((3-1)(4-1)) = 1/(2*3)=1/6. Yes! So P(anc(2,3) and anc(2,4)) = 1/6 = 1/((u-1)(v-1))? Here u=3, v=4, (u-1)(v-1)=2*3=6. Good.

Another test: i=2, u=4, v=5? N=5. Probability 1/((4-1)(5-1)) = 1/12? Let's check known results. I think the joint probability is indeed 1/((u-1)(v-1)) for i < u < v. Let's derive properly. For random recursive tree, the ancestors of a vertex j are a random subset of {1,...,j-1}, but with size distribution. The probability that a specific i < j is an ancestor of j: Consider the insertion process. When j is added, it chooses a parent uniformly from {1,...,j-1}. The ancestors of j are exactly the ancestors of its parent. So the set of ancestors of j is the same as the set of ancestors of parent(j). Since parent(j) is uniform in {1,...,j-1}, the probability that i is an ancestor of j is the probability that i is an ancestor of the parent of j. This is (1/(j-1)) * sum_{k=1}^{j-1} P(anc(i,k)). So P(anc(i,j)) = (1/(j-1)) * Σ_{k=1}^{j-1} P(anc(i,k)). For i=1, P(anc(1,j))=1. For i>1, P(anc(i,i))=1. Let's solve: For fixed i, for j>i, P(anc(i,j)) = (1/(j-1)) * Σ_{k=i}^{j-1} P(anc(i,k)) because if k<i, i cannot be ancestor of k. So for j>i, let a_j = P(anc(i,j)). Then a_j = (1/(j-1)) * Σ_{k=i}^{j-1} a_k, with a_i = 1.

j=6: a_6 = (1/5) * (1 + 1/2 + 1/2 + 1/2) = (1/5)*(2.5) = 1/2.

What about i=3, j=5? Let's solve recurrence for general i. For fixed i, for j≥i, a_j = P(anc(i,j)). We have a_i = 1. For j>i, a_j = (1/(j-1)) * Σ_{k=i}^{j-1} a_k. Let's try to find closed form. We can use the fact that in a random recursive tree, the ancestors of a vertex are a random set. There is a known result: The probability that i is an ancestor of both u and v is equal to the probability that i is the LCA of u and v, plus the probability that i is an ancestor of the LCA. Actually, the event that i is an ancestor of both u and v is the union of the events that i is the LCA, or i is an ancestor of the LCA. But since we are in a tree, if i is an ancestor of both, then i is on the path from the LCA to the root. So the probability that i is an ancestor of both u and v is the probability that the LCA of u and v is a descendant of i (i.e., LCA is in the subtree of i). This is equivalent to: the LCA of u and v is a descendant of i. The LCA of u and v is a random vertex. The probability that the LCA is exactly w is known for random recursive trees. Then J(i; u,v) = P( LCA(u,v) is a descendant of i ). Because if LCA is w, then i is an ancestor of both iff i is an ancestor of w (i.e., w is in the subtree of i). So J(i; u,v) = P( LCA(u,v) ∈ subtree(i) ). And we need this for i < u and i < v (otherwise 0). For i ≥ u or i ≥ v, J=0.

Given the complexity, there must be a simpler solution. Let's re-read the problem statement carefully. "There is an integer sequence A = (A_2, A_3, ..., A_N). Also, for an integer sequence P = (P_2, P_3, ..., P_N) where 1 ≤ P_i ≤ i-1, define the weighted tree T(P) with N vertices, rooted at vertex 1... You are given Q queries. The i-th query is: You are given integers u_i and v_i. For each of the possible (N-1)! sequences P, take the tree T(P) and consider the distance between vertices u_i and v_i in this tree. Output the sum, modulo 998244353, of these distances over all T(P)."

This is a problem from a competitive programming contest (likely AtCoder). The constraints are N, Q up to 2e5. The solution must be O((N+Q) log N) or similar.

Let's think about the sum of distances over all trees. Since the sum is linear in the edge weights, we can precompute for each edge e (which is identified by the child vertex i) the number of trees where e is on the path between u and v. So we need for each query (u,v) and each i, the number of trees where the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the u–v path. This is a combinatorial count.

Let's consider the random recursive tree model. There is a known result: The probability that the path between u and v passes through the edge (i,parent(i)) is 0 if i ≤ min(u,v) or something? So i can be u. If i = v, i is on the path, edge is on path. So i can be u or v. Also i can be any vertex between u and v? Actually, i can be any vertex that is an ancestor of exactly one. So i can be any vertex except the root? The root 1 has no parent edge. So i ≥ 2.

Now, the number of trees where the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the u–v path is equal to the number of trees where i is an ancestor of exactly one of u,v. We can compute this by considering the parent of i. Let j be the parent of i. The edge (j,i) is on the path iff i is an ancestor of exactly one of u,v, and parent(i)=j. But note: if i is an ancestor of exactly one of u,v, then parent(i) must be on the path as well? Actually, if i is on the path, its parent is the next vertex on the path toward the LCA. So the edge (j,i) is on the path. So we just need i to be an ancestor of exactly one of u,v. The parent j is then determined by the tree structure, but for the edge to be on the path, it is necessary and sufficient that i is an ancestor of exactly one of u,v. So the number of trees where the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path is exactly the number of trees where i is an ancestor of exactly one of u,v. This does not depend on j! Wait, is that true? In a given tree, if i is an ancestor of exactly one of u,v, then the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path. So the event "edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path" is exactly the event "i is an ancestor of exactly one of u,v". So the number of trees with this edge on the path is exactly the number of trees where i is an ancestor of exactly one of u,v. So we just need to count trees where i is an ancestor of exactly one of u,v.

Let's denote T(i; u,v) = number of trees where i is an ancestor of exactly one of u,v. Then the answer for query (u,v) is Σ_{i=2}^N A_i * T(i; u,v).

Now, we can compute T(i; u,v) using combinatorial arguments. The total number of trees is (N-1)!. We can think of the tree as being built by attaching vertices 2..N in order. The ancestry of i is determined by the parent choices of i and its descendants. The event that i is an ancestor of u is equivalent to: the path from u to root passes through i. This is equivalent to: parent(u) is in the subtree of i, or u = i. Since u is a specific vertex, we can count the number of trees where i is an ancestor of u. We already know that P(anc(i,u)) = 1/i for i < u, 0 for i > u, 1 for i = u. So the number of trees where i is an ancestor of u is (N-1)! / i for i < u, (N-1)! for i = u, 0 for i > u.

Now, we need the number of trees where i is an ancestor of both u and v. Let's denote J(i; u,v) = number of trees where i is an ancestor of both u and v. Then T(i; u,v) = (number of trees where i anc u) + (number of trees where i anc v) - 2 * J(i; u,v). This holds for all i. For i = u, T(u; u,v) = (N-1)! + 0 - 2 * J(u; u,v). But J(u; u,v) = number of trees where u is anc of both u and v = number of trees where u is anc of v = (N-1)! / v (since u < v). So T(u; u,v) = (N-1)! - 2 * (N-1)!/v? That would be negative if v < 2? Wait, for i = u, P(anc(u,u)) = 1, so number of trees = (N-1)!. P(anc(u,v)) = 1/v. So T = (N-1)! + (N-1)!/v - 2 J. But J = number of trees where u anc both = number of trees where u anc v = (N-1)!/v. So T = (N-1)! + (N-1)!/v - 2 (N-1)!/v = (N-1)! - (N-1)!/v. This matches p_u = 1 - 1/v. So it's correct. For i = v, P(anc(v,v)) = 1, P(anc(v,u)) = 0, J = 0 (since v > u, v cannot be anc of u). So T = (N-1)! + 0 - 0 = (N-1)!. So p_v = 1. Correct.

So the key is to compute J(i; u,v) = number of trees where i is an ancestor of both u and v. For i < min(u,v), we need this. For i ≥ min(u,v), J = 0 except when i = min(u,v) and i is anc of the other, but we already handled i=u or i=v.

So we need J(i; u,v) for i < u and i < v. Let's assume i < u < v. We want the number of trees on N vertices where i is an ancestor of both u and v. As we argued, this is equivalent to: u and v are both in the subtree of i. The subtree of i is a random recursive tree. The number of trees where u and v are in the subtree of i can be computed by considering the tree on the set of vertices that are descendants of i, and the tree on the rest. The descendants of i are a set of size that varies. The probability that u and v are in the subtree of i is the probability that in the random recursive tree on {1,...,v}, u and v are descendants of i. This probability depends only on v, not on N, as long as N ≥ v. And we can compute it using the insertion process.

Let's use generating functions or a different approach. We want to compute T(i) efficiently for all i. Since N is up to 2e5, we can precompute factorials and compute the sum in O(1) per i if we find a closed form, or O(N) per i is too slow. We need O(N) total for all i, or O(N log N).

Note that \binom{M-2}{k-2} = \frac{(M-2)!}{(k-2)! (M-k)!}.
So the term is \frac{(M-2)!}{(k-2)! (M-k)!} * k! * (M - k)! = (M-2)! * k! / (k-2)! = (M-2)! * k (k-1).

Maybe we can compute T(i) using a different approach. Since N and Q are up to 2e5, we can precompute T(i) for all i in O(N) time using a recurrence. We have T(i) = number of trees where i is an ancestor of both u and v (for any u,v > i). This is a function of i and N. We can try to find a recurrence for T(i). 

Let's consider the random recursive tree. The probability that i is an ancestor of both u and v is J(i; u,v). We can use the fact that the subtree of i is a random recursive tree. Given the time and complexity, and since this is a coding problem, there must be a known simpler solution. Let's search for a different approach.

We have the formula for the sum of distances in terms of A_i and c_i(u,v). We can precompute for all i the value of c_i(u,v) as a function of u and v. Notice that c_i(u,v) is the number of trees where the edge (i,parent(i)) is on the path. This is equal to the number of trees where i separates u and v. In a tree, the probability that a given edge separates two random vertices is proportional to the product of the sizes of the components. But here the tree is random and u,v are fixed. However, there is a known result: In a random recursive tree, the probability that the path between u and v goes through the edge (i,parent(i)) is:
- 0 if i ≤ min(u,v) and i is not the LCA? No.
Actually, we can use the fact that the random recursive tree is a uniform random tree among all recursive trees. The number of recursive trees where a given edge separates u and v can be computed by the following: The edge (i,parent(i)) separates u and v iff u and v are in different components of the tree when the edge is removed. The components are the subtree of i and the rest. So we need the number of trees where u is in the subtree of i and v is not, plus vice versa. This is exactly the number of trees where exactly one of u,v is in the subtree of i. The number of trees where u is in the subtree of i is (N-1)! / i. The number where both are in the subtree of i is T(i; u,v). So c_i(u,v) = (N-1)!/i + (N-1)!/i - 2 T(i; u,v) for i < min(u,v). For i between u and v, it's (N-1)!/i. For i > max, 0.

So we need T(i; u,v). We saw that T(i; u,v) depends on u and v in a way that is not simply a function of i. But wait, for a fixed i, the event that both u and v are in the subtree of i depends on the relative order of u and v. However, we can use the fact that the random recursive tree is a uniform random tree. The probability that both u and v are in the subtree of i is the probability that in the tree on {1,...,v}, i is an ancestor of both. This probability can be computed as: P( i is anc u and i is anc v ) = P( i is anc u ) * P( i is anc v | i is anc u ). We know P( i anc u ) = 1/i. Given that i is anc u, what is P( i anc v )? The subtree of i is a random recursive tree. Given that u is in the subtree, the subtree of i is a random recursive tree on a set that includes u. The probability that v is also in the subtree is the probability that v attaches to the subtree. This might be 1/(i+1) or something. But we saw it's not constant.

This is equal to Σ_{e} w_e * (number of trees where e is on the path). The number of trees where an edge e is on the path between u and v is equal to the number of trees where the components separated by e contain u and v respectively. For the edge (i,parent(i)), the components are the subtree of i (size k) and the rest (size N-k). The number of trees where the subtree of i has size k and contains u (or v) can be computed. This is a standard problem: the number of recursive trees with a given subtree size. 

Let's use the known result: In a random recursive tree, the size of the subtree of a given vertex i has a Borel distribution. Specifically, the probability that the subtree of i has size m (including i) is \frac{1}{i(i+1)...(i+m-2)}? No, the Borel distribution is P(m) = 1/(m(m-1)). (N-k-1)!. So the number of trees where i has exactly k descendants is \binom{N-i}{k} k! (N - k - 2)!. Let's test with N=4, i=2. k=2: \binom{2}{2} 2! (4-2-2)! = 1 * 2 * 0! = 2. k=1: \binom{2}{1} 1! (4-1-2)! = 2 * 1 * 1! = 2. k=0: \binom{2}{0} 0! (4-0-2)! = 1 * 1 * 2! = 2. Total = 6. Correct. So the number of trees where i has exactly k descendants is \binom{N-i}{k} k! (N - k - 2)!.

Now, for a fixed i, the number of trees where both u and v are in the subtree of i is the number of trees where i has at least 2 descendants and the set of descendants includes u and v. This can be computed by summing over k ≥ 2 the number of trees where i has exactly k descendants, multiplied by the probability that the set of k descendants includes u and v. Given that i has exactly k descendants, the set of descendants is a uniformly random subset of size k from the N-i available vertices. The probability that it includes both u and v is \binom{N-i-2}{k-2} / \binom{N-i}{k} = \frac{k(k-1)}{(N-i)(N-i-1)}. Let's use the fact that the random recursive tree is a uniform random tree.
